  - `next` moves `activeEntryId`. It skips dead creatures when `skipDefeated` is on, a dead player character's row included, since it has no turn to take, and increments `round` on wrap.
  - It records where the creature whose turn starts stands as the encounter's `turnStart` (v1), with the op's seq naming the turn (section 9.2), after any reveal below, so a creature revealed on its turn has its spot marked as its turn line is: `seen` only where players see it stand (section 7.3). A removal that passes the turn on records the next creature's spot the same way, and writes that turn's `turn` line, since a turn it passes on is a turn like any other: the timer and the areas' reminders start from the line (section 9.3).
  - It clears `reactionUsed` and resets `legendary.actionsUsed` for the entry whose turn starts, and ticks durations. A reveal (below) happens before the tick, so the expiry lines of a creature revealed this turn are marked like its turn line: `seen` when players see where it stands (section 7.3).
  - When `next` lands on a `hidden` entry and `revealHiddenOnTurn` is on, the entry's `hidden` becomes false, and its token's `visibility` becomes `'all'` and `fogExemptAuto` becomes true. That is the meaning of "hidden combatants appear on their first turn": the creature is revealed, and players then see it as they see any creature (the project owner, 2026-10-14: "a monster appearing should then only be visible when players could conceivably see it"). On a fog scene it shows on revealed ground, and on a vision scene while the party sees it. Until then its token and row stay withheld, and players see "Another creature's turn" (section 13.4). `fogExemptAuto` still sets on the reveal and clears when the encounter ends or the entry is removed, so stored ops replay to the state they made, but it no longer lifts the creature above the fog or out of the sight rules. The DM's own `fogExempt` flag is separate and unchanged, and it is the only one the DM's token panel shows. With the setting off, players see "Another creature's turn" until the DM reveals the row.
  - Each `next` pushes the prior encounter, the effects it touched, the legendary actions it reset, and the prior state of any token it revealed onto `encounterHistory`. `prev` pops it, so Previous restores expired effects and used legendary actions exactly and hides a combatant it had revealed, row and token together.
  - `prev` logs a `turn` event with `back: true` for the turn it takes back, marked `seen` from the state before the undo, as that turn's own line was. It names the row whose turn resumes (`to`), with `toSeen` when players who do not own that row could see it as it resumed. Players get `to` only with that mark, or as the row's owners, and never the mark. So when the same turn starts again, the log reads "the DM took back Goblin 2's turn" between the two lines instead of a bare repeat, and a row the step hides again keeps both lines out of the players' log.
  - **A press names the turn it ends** (live play after v1 wave 3). `next` and `prev` carry an optional `from`: the turn the press ends, as its row (`entry`, null for Start before any turn) and its `round`. The round is part of it because a row names a combatant, not a turn: a lone combatant, or the last one standing while `skipDefeated` passes over the dead, acts again in the next round with the same row. When the encounter no longer stands at that row and round, because a player's End Turn and the DM's Next arrived together, two DM devices pressed at once, or a double tap sent a second press, the op changes nothing, writes no line, ticks nothing and reminds no one, and the server takes it without a refusal, so nobody sees an error for a turn that already moved. A player's guarded press only ever ends that player's own turn, and only while that row is in the player's view: one that names another row's turn changes nothing, and so does one that names exactly the turn of the player's own row while the DM hides that row or its token stands on another scene, since the player's tracker shows no turn then and offers no End Turn. Permissions check what the player sees first. Behind a blackout, as with no fight, every press is `gone`, whether a row acts and whatever the press names. While no row the player sees acts (a hidden row's turn, or before Start), every guarded press is taken alike, so none tells which hidden row acts. A late press for a visible turn is taken, and one for the turn of a visible row the player does not own is refused as not their turn. The client names the turn on screen, except that a press while this device still waits on one of the same kind names that one's turn again: the DM's Next is predicted, so a double tap would otherwise name the next turn and move two. A press of the other kind, Previous after Next, names the turn on screen. An op without `from`, as every op stored before it, moves the turn under the old rules.
- Removing an entry, or ending the encounter, re-anchors every duration on that entry to the entry's token or character (section 9.5).
- **Hold and Ready** (v1): a row's options ready an action, the `readied` status with its trigger, lasting until the start of the creature's next turn. "It fired" removes it and sets `reactionUsed`. The DM does this for any row, a player for their own.
- **Turn timer** (v1, `client/features/encounter/timer.ts`): with `turnTimerSec` set, every device counts the acting turn down from when it began. The DM picks off, 30 seconds or 1, 2, 3, 5 or 10 minutes. A room may hold any number of seconds up to 600 (an import or a raw op), and the choices then show that one as well, so the control never reads as unset. The turn's log line records that in the server's time, and a turn whose line the viewer does not hold, such as one that began out of their sight, counts from when the device saw it start. The same row's line from an earlier round is not its start, since a viewer whose log lacks the turns between would otherwise read a turn long over. A line taken back (Previous) is when the turn it names in `to` began again, so it counts only for that row, and only when its op came after the acting turn began (`TurnStart.seq`). A viewer whose line lacks `to`, because they could not see that row resume, or who lacks the turn's start, counts from when the device saw the turn. Players time only turns they can see, so a hidden creature's turn shows no clock. At zero the clock turns red and counts the time past it, and the device of whoever acts (a player for their own creature, the DM for the rest) vibrates or chimes once and shows "Time is up". It never ends a turn: a turn at the table stops for questions and jokes, and one that ended by itself would need taking back.

### 9.5 Durations

- The UI offers "until the start of X's next turn", "until the end of X's next turn", and "N rounds". The reducer turns that into `remaining` crossings of a boundary of the anchor entry, counted from the moment it is applied:
  - "End of X's next turn" is 2 `turnEnd` crossings if it is applied during X's own turn (the current turn's end comes first), and 1 otherwise.
  - "Start of X's next turn" is always 1 `turnStart` crossing.
  - "N rounds" is N `turnStart` crossings of X.
- The anchor is X's initiative entry while X is in the encounter. Outside an encounter, or when X has no entry, the anchor is X's token or character instead, and the duration is paused.
- When X is added to an encounter, every paused duration anchored to X's token or character is re-anchored to the new entry and starts counting from there.
- When X's entry is removed or the encounter ends, durations anchored to it are re-anchored to X's token or character and pause, keeping their `remaining`.
- A paused duration shows "paused (outside combat)" on the effect chip. If X never joins an encounter, it stays paused until the DM removes it, which is the same as ordinary play at a real table.
- Expiry adds a log event, filtered by the normal log projection.
- **Save ends** (v1 wave 7, docs/design-v1-roll-options.md 6.1): an effect with `save` ends on a save at the end of each of its creature's own turns. `encounter.next` writes one `saveEnds` line per such effect on each creature the ended row stands for, after the tick, so an effect expiring in the same step gets none, and sets the effect's `due` to that line's id, pending. A new turn end replaces the occurrence. `due` settles as resolved or dismissed by closing its linked request, or by the DM's `actor.saveDue` when no request is linked, and a `roll.ask` with `ends` is refused unless its occurrence is pending, so a settled save is never asked again. A pass removes the effect. Previous restores the effects as they were, `due` included.

### 9.6 HP and life state

`rules/hp.ts` owns these transitions. The reducer applies them, and tests cover each one.

- **Damage** takes temporary HP first, then current HP, never below 0.
- **Reaching 0 HP:**
  - A `pc` becomes `dying`, gains Unconscious, and its death saves reset to 0 and 0.
  - An `npc` or `monster` becomes `dead` when `monstersDieAtZero` is on. Otherwise it follows the PC rules: `dying`, Unconscious, and death saves that the DM enters.
  - Unconscious belongs to dying and stable. A creature that becomes `dead`, by any route, loses the Unconscious that 0 HP brought, and keeps any Unconscious the DM applied by hand.
  - An `object` stays at 0 with no state change.
- **Massive damage:** if the damage left over after reaching 0 is at least the creature's HP maximum, it becomes `dead` at once.
- **Damage at 0 HP** to a dying PC adds one failed death save, or two when the DM marks it a critical. Damage at least equal to max HP kills. The 2024 rules count the damage taken, so damage that temporary HP absorbs still adds the failure, and the whole hit counts toward max HP. The same holds while `stable`.
- **Death saves:** three successes make the creature `stable`: still Unconscious, no more saves, and both counters reset to 0. Three failures make it `dead`.
- **Damage while `stable`:** it becomes `dying` again with one failed death save, or two on a critical. Damage at least equal to max HP kills.
- **Healing:**
  - From 0 for a `dying` or `stable` creature, it sets life to `alive`, removes the Unconscious that came from 0 HP, and clears death saves.
  - Healing a `dead` creature, or a set of its current HP above 0, brings it back with that HP while the room's `healRevives` is on, the default (docs/design-v1-combat-flow.md 3.4). Off, healing does nothing to the dead, and only the DM's `actor.life` (revive) changes that. Ops stored before op version 5 replay with the old rule.
  - The DM's revive gives a creature that is not alive exactly 1 HP (docs/design-v1-combat-flow.md 3.5).
  - A companion that is an effect, or any token at max HP 0, has no HP to show (`hpLess`, docs/design-v1-combat-flow.md 3.6.6).
- **Concentration:**
  - Gaining Incapacitated ends `concentrating`, with a log line. In 2024, Paralyzed, Petrified, Stunned and Unconscious all include Incapacitated, so gaining any of them ends it too.
  - Damage while concentrating adds a prompt with the DC `min(30, max(10, floor(damage / 2)))`. The player reports pass or fail.
- **Rests** (v1, `rules/rest.ts`), from SRD 5.2.1's Rules Glossary. Either rest needs at least 1 HP, alive. A short rest adds the HP the player's Hit Point Dice gave, typed by the player (the rest rolls nothing and counts no dice), up to the maximum. A long rest restores all HP, ends temporary HP (they last until a long rest), lowers Exhaustion one level and removes it at 0 with a log line marked hidden when the condition was, and resets Legendary Resistance (X/Day uses come back after a long rest) and legendary actions. Spell slots, class features and a reduced HP maximum are left to the sheet. Each rest writes a `rest` line, with numbers only for viewers who see the creature's exact HP. A rest is allowed during a fight, since the table may skip ahead or rest a creature far from it, and the rest section then notes that rolling Initiative stops a rest (SRD 5.2.1).
- **`hpLabel(actor)`:**
  - Dead when `dead`.
  - Dying when `dying` or `stable`.
  - Bloodied at or below half (`cur <= floor(max / 2)`).
  - Healthy otherwise.

### 9.7 The wall set (v1 wave 3)

`walls.ts` derives one wall set per scene, which every rule reads, and `segments.ts` holds its exact geometry. Addendum section 2.1 has the full reasoning.

- **Stored segments.** The set starts from the builder's effective edges under the scene's automatic walls rule (section 12), with plain walls merged into runs along each grid line and doors and windows kept one edge each, so a door's state never changes the segments. Each pillar cell adds its square, inset 9/64 cell so its corners lie on the 1/64 lattice, and the free walls come last. Every end lies on the lattice. A stored free wall that breaks the protocol's rules (off the lattice, longer than 8 cells, or of zero length) is dropped as bad data, as malformed keys are.
- **What each segment blocks.** Areas keep the MVP rule. Movement and sight are tagged as players see the build, a secret door not yet found being a wall to them, and since v1 wave 4 the movement rule (section 9.8) and sight (section 9.10) read them.

  | Feature | Areas | Movement (wave 4) | Sight (wave 4) |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | Wall (edge or free) | blocks | blocks | blocks |
  | Window | blocks | blocks | open |
  | Door, open | open | open | open |
  | Door, closed or locked | blocks | blocks | blocks |
  | Secret door not yet found | its real state | blocks | blocks |
  | Pillar square | open, as before | blocks | blocks |
  | Raised edge (v1 wave 4, section 9.9) | open | blocks a walking token unless a stair connects it, while `blockMovement` is on | blocks an eye below its top |

  A fourth tag marks every piece that would block sight with every door closed and every secret door hidden, and every raised edge whatever its height, so wave 4's sight cap can count them whatever the door states.
- **The split.** The area sweep needs segments that never cross, and free walls cross grid walls, each other and pillar squares. So `splitSegments` cuts every segment wherever a free wall crosses or touches it, and partitions every line where collinear segments overlap, each piece carrying the tags of every segment over it. Grid segments meet only at grid points, where walls.ts already cut them, so a scene without free walls needs no split at all. Any subset of the pieces is crossing-free, so the pieces that block areas go to walls.ts's existing sweep as they are. A split point is computed once and shared by the two pieces it ends, so a split never opens a gap.
- **Exact arithmetic.** segments.ts uses only `+`, `-`, `*`, `/` and comparisons (with `Math.floor`, `abs`, `min` and `max`), which IEEE 754 rounds the same way in every engine, and a test fails if it or anything it imports calls `atan2`, `sin`, `cos`, `sqrt`, `hypot`, `pow` or `exp`. Its orientation predicate (`orient`) is Shewchuk's adaptive arithmetic from the `robust-predicates` package, pinned at 3.0.3 (Unlicense, no dependencies), which gives the exact sign of a cross product for any doubles. Touch, side and incidence decisions read the stored segments, whose ends lie on the lattice, never the split pieces, whose split points are rounded. Wave 4's sight needs this exactness, since its "seen" marks are stored facts that replay must rebuild bit for bit. Areas keep walls.ts's sweep, since area results are never stored.
- **The split's caps.** A counting pass (`countSplit`) counts the candidate pairs (a free wall and another segment that share a cell row and overlap along x, every pair the pass compares, whether or not the two meet, so the cap bounds its work) and the pieces their crossings would add, without building any piece, and stops as soon as a count passes its cap: 100,000 pairs and 5,000 added pieces per scene (section 10.5), the pieces cap brought down from 10,000 when the densest build at every cap ran past the derive budget. The server's limits step runs it (`splitCheck`) on every scene whose walls version an op moved, restores included, and on a scene an op created, such as a duplicate. Over a cap it refuses with `quota` in the scene's terms: "this scene's walls would cross too often: they would split into more than 5,000 extra pieces", or, for pairs, "would cross and meet too often: more than 100,000 pairs of walls to check". The room import runs it on every scene (section 16.4), and the Universal VTT import runs it before sending anything (section 14.9). So every stored scene splits within the caps, and a dense pile of free walls or a lattice of crossing walls is refused at the op that would make it. A device that holds a set over the caps for a moment (a client's optimistic view, or the server's staged state before the check) splits it without its free walls, so no derive does more work than the caps allow.
- **The caps on pillars, doors and windows, and drawn edges.** The split's caps bound pairs and pieces, not the stored segments the derive and the counting pass walk, nor the entries the derive merges from every chunk on every derive. A pillar cell is four stored segments. A door or window is one that never merges into a run. Every drawn edge, walls and removed automatic walls included, is an entry in the derive's lookups whatever it holds, and a wall drawn on all 80,000 edges of a 200 x 200 scene derives at about 18 ms on a development laptop. Before these caps only the scene's byte size bounded any of them. So a scene holds at most 1,000 pillar cells, 20,000 drawn edges, and within those 1,000 doors and windows, grid edges and free walls together, secret doors included (section 10.5). `checkLimits` counts them for every op that writes a scene's chunks, restores included, and the room import counts them on every scene. The caps came after rooms that may hold more, so with the state before the op each refuses only an op that raises its count past the cap ("this scene can hold at most 1,000 pillars"): such a room still loads, and its other edits, and removing pillars, doors or edges, still work. A scene over any of these caps cannot be duplicated until it is under the cap: the copy is a new scene, so its count rises from none, and the duplicate is refused with the cap's message like any op that raises the count. An undo or redo that would bring back a scene past a cap is refused the same way, such as undoing the deletion of a scene over one. The import, with no state before it, refuses a scene over any cap. The Universal VTT import paints no pillars, and refuses a map with more grid edges, or more door and window edges, than the caps before sending anything, since a portal or wall along a grid line becomes one edge per cell. The counts are kept per chunk object, so an op recounts only the chunks it wrote. The bench (below) sized all of them, the pieces cap included, on the densest build at every cap, and a modest 4-core server, which ran it at about 1.4 times this laptop's times, left room for 20,000 drawn edges: enough for a large imported map whose walls snap to the grid, one edge per cell.
- **Caches follow identity** (`sceneWalls`). A scene's walls version is reused when the size guard refuses a staged op, so a cache keyed on it could serve walls the room never kept. Entities are replaced, never changed, so the list of a scene's chunk objects, with its automatic walls rule and bounds, names exactly one wall set. Each scene keeps two sets, for the committed state and the staged one (or a client's confirmed and optimistic views). Scene IDs are chosen by clients, so two rooms can share one, which costs a miss and never a wrong answer. The split, the area pieces and the per-cell index are worked out only when first asked for, so the counting pass never pays for a split it does not need.
- **Used by wave 4.** segments.ts also holds clipping to a sweep's box, a per-cell index of the stored segments, and the bounded step that moves an eye, a light or an area's origin off a wall, tested against an independent ray cast for whole-scene, culled and light boxes. Areas use the step and the index (section 9.3), the movement rule the index (section 9.8), and sight all three (section 9.10). Since v1 wave 4 the set also holds raised edges with their tops, and gives each eye height its own sight set: the sight pieces plus the raised edges whose top is above the eye, a subset of the crossing-free split, so it goes to the sweep as it is. Moves reuse the derived set: it is derived again only after a build or a door edit.
- **Performance.** `scripts/bench-walls.ts` runs the geometry rows of the addendum's performance gate (4.3.6) on scenes at the split's caps and just past them: the counting pass on an op that writes geometry, and deriving, splitting and indexing a scene's walls. Its scenes include the worst cases found for the pass: walls on every lattice line of one cell row, coincident walls on one line, vertical free walls nine rows tall, and the densest grid a build can have (every other cell painted, so every edge is a wall) with pillars, drawn edges, and doors and windows at their caps, under free walls at the split's caps. A wall drawn on every edge of the scene shows what the drawn edge cap refuses. The refused column counts the limits step's caps as well as the split's.

### 9.8 Movement and doors (v1 wave 4)

`shared/rules/movement.ts`, called through `shared/ruleHooks.ts` by permissions and by the client's drag. Addendum 4.1 has the full reasoning, and its section 9 says which questions the project owner decided.

- **A permission rule, never a reducer one.** `canApply` refuses a player's `token.move` or `token.moveMany` whose leg is not allowed on a scene with `blockMovement` (section 8.1). Replay runs the reducer alone (section 16.2), so stored moves replay as they were made, and no op version changed. The DM's moves are never stopped, and a player who may move other tokens (`playersMoveAny`) is stopped the same way when moving them. The leg test is skipped when the move changes neither `x` nor `y`. Blocking is a table aid, not an anti-cheat measure: a player can still raise a token's elevation and fly it over a ledge, and the DM sees the elevation badge.
- **The movement set is the stored segments**, never the split pieces, through the per-cell index (section 9.7): walls, closed and locked doors, windows, secret doors not yet found, pillar squares, and raised edges for a token they stop (section 9.9). So a player's projected build gives exactly the server's blocking set, which a test checks for every secret-door and lock state.
- **The core.** A creature fits through an opening sized for one size smaller, as SRD 5.2.1's narrow opening allows. Per axis, the core's side is `max(0, side - 1 - 1/32)` cells, so a Large creature's core passes a 5 ft opening, a Huge one's 10 ft and a Gargantuan one's 15 ft. Medium and smaller creatures, and custom sizes up to 1 1/32 cells a side, are a point.
- **A leg is allowed** when the core, swept along the straight path from the start center to the end center, touches no segment of the set. Touching counts. A segment the core already touches where the leg starts is ignored along the path's first straight part only while the part moves the center away from that segment's line, so a token can step off a wall it overlaps but never through it. For a core larger than a point, when the straight path is blocked, the paths through start + d1 and end + d2 are tried, each `d` one of the nine shifts of 0 or half a cell per axis in a fixed order, so a Large creature lines up with a 5 ft opening. No shift can jump a wall. Everything uses `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, comparisons and the exact orientation predicate, so a phone's drag and the server's `canApply` always agree.
- **Squeezing** (the project owner, 2026-10-14, the addendum's question 12). A move with `squeeze` is accepted, from anyone, only when four checks hold at its end: the token has a side of at least 2 cells, its squeezed footprint (each side of at least 2 cells one cell shorter, centered on its center, never snapped) lies on cell boundaries, it stands in a real narrow space, and its core touches no blocker. The narrow space is the owner's rule after the build: along an axis the squeeze shrinks, some cross-section of the full footprint level with the squeezed one meets a blocker on each side of the core inside the full footprint and none across the core, and no blocker passes through the squeezed footprint (`pinches`). So a corridor's walls, at its dead end too, and an open door's frame pinch, while open ground beside one wall never squeezes, a corridor's outside wall and a closed door in its frame included. Ledges count where they would stop the token's leg, judged from where it stood before the move. While `squeeze` is set, every rule that reads the footprint (areas, distances, the ruler, range rings, fog, sight, drag frames and the "seen" marks) uses the squeezed one, which lies on cell boundaries. Any move without the flag clears it, and so does a `token.update` of size, scene or position. A squeeze leaves the token's height and eye as they were.
- **Landings** (`landing`). Where a drop is stopped, the token lands on the last spot along its own snap grid where it may stand. A squeezing creature dropped in a narrow opening tries the nine half-cell shifts and keeps the first where the core fits, the leg is allowed and the squeeze checks pass, and the client tries the squeezed spot under the finger before those (section 14.13).
- **Teleport here** (the project owner, 2026-10-14, question 15). `token.move` with `teleport: 'wall' | 'ledge'` skips the wall and ledge test and nothing else: the player must still be allowed to move the token, and a player's teleport keeps the full footprint inside the scene, with `blockMovement` off too. The DM's are not checked. On the active creature's turn the reducer adds its leg already marked `uncounted` (section 9.2), and in every case it writes the DM-only log line `{ type: 'teleport', tokenId, past }`. The flag is in the stored op, so replay marks the same leg and writes the same line. Undo is in section 7.7, and the client's flow, which takes the stopped walk back first, in section 14.13.
- **Doors.** `door.set` (section 8.1). A player's door must be within reach of a token of their own on the scene: the larger of 5 ft and one cell, by the room's distance rule as its ruler measures (`legFeet`), in three dimensions, from the token's footprint and height to either cell beside the door at the ground there, so a creature on a cell beside the door always reaches it and one flying 30 ft up does not. A free door's two sides are the cells 1/256 cell off its midpoint along its normal. A straight line from the footprint's center to the door's midpoint must also cross no movement blocker but the door (`lineClear`), touching counting, except a segment that meets the line only at the door's midpoint, and ledges counting for a walker. That reach is the owner's choice following the addendum's recommendation for question 5, which the project owner kept on 2026-10-14. `found` marks any door discovered. A second tap closes the door again, so a player's door needs no undo, and no log line or DM notice is written.
- **Performance.** A leg test walks the cells the swept core covers and the few segments each holds: well under 1 ms, a blocked Large creature's 81 shifted paths included, on the server and on a phone during a drag (`scripts/bench-walls.ts` and `scripts/bench-core4.ts` measure it at the caps).

### 9.9 Heights and stairs (v1 wave 4)

`shared/rules/heights.ts`, with the chunk codec in `shared/heightCodec.ts`. Addendum 4.2 has the reasoning.

- **Ground.** A cell's ground is its painted height in whole feet, -100 to 200, 0 when absent. A footprint's ground is the highest ground under it. A token stands on the ground when its elevation equals the ground under it, flies above it, and is inside it below, which is legitimate (burrowing), so no rule raises an elevation on its own.
- **Landings write the ground.** Placing a token, and every drop of a token that was on the ground, sets its elevation to the ground where it lands. A flier lands at its elevation or the ground there, whichever is higher, and a token inside the ground keeps its elevation. The client puts the elevation in the op, so replay never depends on the rule. A scene's duplicate and a room import copy elevations as stored. A token's Duplicate, on its action bar, lands the copy as a drop of the original would land there, so a copy of a creature on a platform placed on the floor stands on the floor.
- **A height paint lifts tokens.** `build.height` lifts or lowers every token on the scene that stood on the ground there to the new ground, in the same op, with no leg added to a path. A scene holds at most 500 tokens, which bounds the work. Erase and Clear take heights the same way: a token that stood on the ground they took lands on what is left, in the same op and undo step, while a flier keeps its elevation and so does a token inside the ground.
- **Stairs.** A cell is a stair when its terrain is Stairs or its mark is not `.`, and under every automatic walls rule a stair mark counts as a Stairs cell (section 12). Its direction is a mark of `n`, `e`, `s` or `w` first. Without one, it points to its one orthogonal neighbor with higher ground, if exactly one, a neighbor outside the scene never counting. Failing that, repeated until nothing changes, it takes the direction of the adjacent directed stairs straight up or down its line when they agree, so a stair run passes its direction down. Otherwise it has none. The passes are bounded and run in a fixed order, so the answer is the same everywhere.
- **Raised edges.** An edge between two cells of the scene with different ground is raised, its top the higher ground. A directed stair connects the edges on its up and down sides, whatever the heights. Every other raised edge is a ledge. Raised edges are derived with the walls (section 9.7).
- **Movement** (while `blockMovement` is on, section 9.8). A token on the ground at a leg's start walks, and a ledge stops it in either direction: stairs only, both ways, the addendum's recommendation for questions 2 and 13, which the project owner kept on 2026-10-14. A flier is stopped at a ledge only when the lower of its start and end elevation is below the top. A stair connection passes walkers and fliers. A token inside the ground is not tested against ledges, and walls still stop it.
- **Sight** (section 9.10). A viewer's eye is at its elevation plus its height, and a raised edge whose top is above the eye blocks it. A token inside the ground has no eye. So a Medium creature on the ground sees over a 5 ft ledge but not onto a 10 ft platform, and a creature in a 10 ft pit cannot see out. A creature is in sight only when sight covers its footprint, whatever its elevation, so a flier over a platform higher than the party's eyes is hidden from below: the addendum's simple rule (question 3, kept by the project owner on 2026-10-14), and the DM's always-visible mark covers the dramatic cases.

### 9.10 Vision and light (v1 wave 4)

`shared/rules/vision.ts`, a pure function the server calls through `shared/ruleHooks.ts`. Addendum 4.3 has the reasoning, its sources and the performance gate. Section 13.6 says how sight combines with fog and reaches players.

- **Party vision** (the project owner, 2026-10-14, the addendum's question 1). What players see is the union of what every party source sees, the same for every player.
  - A **party member** is a token a player owns, directly or through its character, or one the DM marked `partyVision`. Every member is shown to players, whatever its state.
  - A **source** is a member on the scene that is visible, a creature (`pc`, `npc` or `monster`, or any kind marked `partyVision`), alive and not Unconscious (a missing actor counts as down), and not inside the ground. At most 12 lend sight per scene, in a fixed order: player characters, then other player-owned creatures, then creatures the DM marked, each by token ID. The cap is taken before the step off a wall, so a source standing on a wall keeps its place. Past it, members stay shown and lend nothing, and the DM's Vision settings say how many.
- **Senses** come from the actor's senses line: `darkvision N`, `blindsight N` and `truesight N`, case-insensitive, feet, the largest of each, each capped at 150 ft (`parseSenses`). Tremorsense is not sight. Owners edit their senses line and their token's light, which the project owner decided is trusted, capped at the SRD's maximums (question 11), and the DM sees what owners set.
- **Light.** The scene's level, bright, dim or dark, is the base. A lit light raises points it reaches to bright within its bright radius and to dim within its dim band beyond, inside its own visibility polygon when it casts shadows. A free light sits 5 ft above the ground under it, a token's light at the token's eye. A DM-hidden token's light is off for players, and a token withheld only because the party cannot see it still lights its surroundings. In a bright scene lights change nothing and are skipped. Light radii and sense ranges are horizontal circles that ignore height.
- **What a source sees.** Its line-of-sight polygon from its eye, swept over its eye height's sight set (section 9.7), and at each point inside it, by the light there. Bright light is bright. Dim light is dim, or bright within darkvision. Darkness is not seen, grey within darkvision, or dim within truesight. Within blindsight a point is at least grey, light or not, walls still blocking. A Blinded source keeps only blindsight.
- **Eyes and lights on a wall.** The sweep ignores a segment through its origin, so an eye within 1/4096 cell of a stored sight segment steps off it with the bounded step of segments.ts (`stepOffWalls`), along sixteen directions at 1/64, 1/128 and 1/256 cell, never across a wall and never along one, keeping to its own side of a wall it is only near. If none of the 48 tries qualifies, the source lends no sight and the DM's Vision settings list it as standing on a wall, and a light fails closed the same way. A source in a 1/64-cell gap between two walls sees nothing.
- **The party's sight** is, per sub-cell of the fog's 4 x 4 grid, the best level any source has there: 0 not seen, 1 grey, 2 dim, 3 bright, rasterized at sub-cell centers by spans per row, circles by an integer search. **A creature is in sight** when any sub-cell whose center lies inside its footprint rectangle at its stored position (the squeezed one while it squeezes) has a level above 0, so a Tiny creature behind a free wall in its own cell stays hidden. Projection, drag frames and the "seen" marks all use this one predicate.
- **Exact arithmetic.** The "seen" marks are stored facts that replay rebuilds, so vision.ts, segments.ts and heights.ts use only `+`, `-`, `*`, `/` and comparisons, sorting the sweep's ends by an exact half-plane and cross-product order instead of `atan2`, and a test fails if they or anything they import call `atan2`, `sin`, `cos`, `sqrt`, `hypot`, `pow` or `exp`. Golden-bit tests pin the output for fixed scenes, and an independent brute-force ray cast checks it on random scenes with crossing free walls, pillars and raised edges.
- **Sweep boxes.** In a bright or dim scene a source's sweep covers the scene. In a dark one it covers the source's senses and every lit area, and a Blinded source's covers its blindsight alone. A light's sweep covers its radius. Every sweep takes every segment that reaches its box, clipped to the box.
- **Incremental and exact.** A cache per scene keeps each source's and light's polygon with the inputs it came from: position, eye height, range, the actor's conditions, life and senses, and the scene's walls by identity. A polygon is worked out again only when its own inputs change or a sight piece that differs meets its box, so the incremental answer always equals a cold one, which tests check. A light's polygon is worked out only when some source's box reaches its lit area. The caches are capped at 64 MB across the server, dropped least recently used by scene, two entries per scene for the committed and the staged state.
- **Tiles.** Sight reaches clients as 16 x 16-cell tiles of 2 bits per sub-cell, least significant first, in row order, as base64, or `{f}` for a tile all at one level, sub-cells past the scene 0 (section 7.3). No client works out sight: a client's optimistic replay reads the tiles it holds, and a scene with none counts as nothing seen.
- **Caps.** At most 12 sources, 100 free lights and 20 lit token lights, light radii of 120 ft plus 120 ft and senses of 150 ft (and 24 and 30 cells, which bind only below 5 ft per cell), and 6,000 potential sight pieces per scene (section 10.5). `scripts/bench-vision.ts` and `scripts/bench-server4.ts` run the addendum's performance gate at the caps, bright and dark, with heights, and `scripts/bench-sight-overlay.ts` the client's overlay rows.

### 9.11 Roll requests (v1 wave 5)

`shared/reducer/rolls.ts`, with `shared/rules/skills.ts` and `shared/rules/rollReminders.ts`. docs/design-v1-dm-tools.md 3.3 has the reasoning and the SRD 5.2.1 rules it follows. Players type the total they rolled, as for initiative, or since v1 wave 7 the server rolls it (`roll.dice`, docs/design-v1-dice.md 5.4 and 7): with the creature's bonus from its numbers (`shared/dice/bonus.ts`), else one the player types, the dice kept on the target, and a save that a visible condition fails recorded as Fail with its total. A rolled answer is final for its player, and the DM allows a reroll or an added die in one tap. Section 14.15 has the cards.

- **What a request asks.** A saving throw or an ability check, with one or two choices the creature picks from, each an ability with, in a check only, an optional skill. Choices are pairs, never two lists, so a skill stays with its ability. A skill fills in its usual ability from the SRD's Skills table (page 9), and the DM may change it. A DC from 1 to 50, or none. Two switches, "Players see the DC" and "Players see pass or fail", both on by default since the request's example names the DC. A note of up to 80 characters.
- **Who it asks.** 1 to 50 creatures, each a token on the player scene or a character, and each played by a player, else `invalid` ("only creatures a player plays can be asked to roll", "a token asked to roll must be on the players' scene"). At most 20 requests are open at once. A token deleted since stays a target until the request closes.
- **Answers.** A player answers for a creature they own now: a total from -50 to 100 with the choice they rolled, or on a save a fail without rolling (SRD page 7), which is recorded with `noRoll` so every device of that player shows it. The DM answers or marks any target, and a bare mark sets the result in place. With a DC and a total the reducer sets the result: pass when the total equals or exceeds the DC. An answer that replaces another keeps the replaced one as `previous`, which only the DM receives.
- **Finality.** When the DC is hidden and pass or fail is shown, an answer is final once it has a result, so a player cannot change totals until the passes and fails give the DC away. A later answer from a player is refused with `forbidden`, "Ask the DM to change it." The DM's `roll.reopen` clears the answer, keeping it as `previous`, for one more. With the DC shown there is nothing to find, and with results hidden nothing to read, so a player may change freely while the request is open.
- **No DC.** The result is only ever a DM's mark or the player's own fail without rolling, so an answer that replaces another clears the old mark into `previous`, unless the new answer supplies its own result.
- **Closing.** `roll.close` removes one request or several. Every answer and mark also writes a `roll` log line with the test and the request's two switches, so the line keeps its rules after the request is gone (section 7.3).
- **Reminders.** For each creature asked, the card quotes the SRD text of its conditions and statuses that bear on what was asked: Paralyzed, Petrified, Stunned and Unconscious failing Strength and Dexterity saves, Restrained, Blinded and Deafened, Poisoned and Frightened, Exhaustion as its number ("Exhaustion 2: the roll is reduced by 4"), the Dodge action, Haste, Rage with its caveats, Bless and Bane, Hex (worded generally, since the effect stores no ability) and Heroic Inspiration. They remind and decide nothing. A condition hidden from players is not in their data, so it never shows.

## 10. Rooms, access and security

### 10.1 Room creation

- `POST /api/rooms` takes `{name, playerPassword, dmName}`, plus a Turnstile token when Turnstile is configured.
- The player password must be at least 8 characters and must not be on a bundled list of the 10,000 most common passwords (from SecLists, MIT license). The form offers a generated passphrase by default: three words joined by hyphens, such as `owlbear-druid-wand`, so it is easy to type on a phone. The words come from a bundled list of 1,595 short, easy-to-spell fantasy words: SRD 5.2.1 creature, class, spell and item names (CC-BY-4.0) plus common English nouns. Three words give about 4.06e9 passphrases (32 bits). Section 10.3 says how long guessing one takes under the join limits.
- It creates the room, makes the creating session's grant a DM grant, and creates a 32-byte DM key (base64url). Only `sha256(key)` and `dm_epoch = 1` are stored. The player password is stored twice: its argon2id hash, which checks every join, and a sealed copy for the DM's player link (section 10.2).
- It returns `{roomId, dmLink: "/r/<roomId>#dm=<key>"}`.
- The UI shows the DM link once, with copy, share and a QR code folded away under "Show a QR code for another DM device", and an "I saved it" checkbox before continuing. Below it, the player link (section 10.2) with copy, share and an open QR code, so the group can join right away. Only the player link's code is on screen at first, so a player at the table cannot scan the wrong one.
- Room IDs are 8 characters of Crockford base32 (40 bits), shown as `XXXX-XXXX`. IDs are not secrets. The password is the gate.
- Limits on open creation:
  - 5 rooms per IP per day (`ROOMS_PER_IP_PER_DAY`).
  - 20 rooms per day across the whole site (`ROOMS_PER_DAY`).
  - A per-room storage quota of 1 GiB (`ROOM_QUOTA_MB`). It counts asset bytes plus the room's snapshot and op rows.
  - A global free-disk guard: uploads and new rooms are refused when the volume has under 2 GiB free.
  - Turnstile on the create form when `TURNSTILE_SECRET` is set.
  - In production, a Turnstile token counts only when Cloudflare reports it solved on the hostname of `PUBLIC_ORIGIN`. This applies to every check: room creation, import and the join challenge.
  - Room import counts as a creation (section 16.4).

### 10.2 Sessions, grants and roles

- A session is an opaque 32-byte random token. Only `sha256(token)` is stored, in `sessions`, with an expiry 180 days after last use.
- Rotating a session (on DM claim and invite redemption) replaces `token_hash` on the same `sessions` row, so the old token stops working at once while the session's grants in every room and its watermarks stay attached.
- The cookie is `__Host-rvtt=<token>; Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Strict; Path=/; Max-Age=15552000`, re-sent with a new `Max-Age` at most once a day when used. Strict works because the SPA's HTML is public and every authenticated call is a same-origin `fetch` or WebSocket. Development over plain `http://localhost` uses the name `rvtt` without `Secure`. The app refuses to start in production with the development cookie settings.
- Room access is a row in `grants (session_id, room_id, member_id, role, epoch)`. A session can hold grants for several rooms.

**Authority comes only from the grant.** The actor for every op and every HTTP action is `(grant.member_id, grant.role)`, read when the socket upgrades and rechecked every 60 s and on every epoch change. `Member.kind` is only a label.

- A `player` grant comes only from joining with the player password. Its `epoch` must equal `rooms.player_epoch`.
- A `dm` grant comes only from creating the room, presenting the DM key, or redeeming a DM device invite. Its `epoch` must equal `rooms.dm_epoch`.
- A grant whose epoch is stale is deleted at the next check, and its sockets close with `4001`.

**Joining as a player** takes two steps, so the roster is never shown before the password:

1. `POST /api/rooms/:id/join` with `{password}`, plus `turnstileToken` when the room asks for the join challenge (section 10.3). On success it returns a join ticket and the list of player members: name and color only, never DM members. The ticket is 32 random bytes, single use, valid 5 minutes, and held in memory together with the room ID, the session that verified the password, and the room's `player_epoch` at that moment.
2. `POST /api/rooms/:id/join/complete` with `{ticket, memberId}` to continue as an existing player, or `{ticket, name, color}` to become a new player. It consumes the ticket only if its room is the room in the path, its session is the calling session, and, inside the same transaction that writes the grant, its `player_epoch` still equals the room's. So a ticket from another room is useless, and changing the password kills every outstanding ticket. It refuses a `memberId` whose member is a DM or was ever one. The server records every member that has held a DM grant (`room_dm_members`), so continuing as a former DM cannot reach the whispers that member sent or received as DM. A room export carries those marks (section 16.4). A `memberId` no longer in the room, most likely one the DM removed while the roster was open, gets `409 member_removed` without using up the ticket, as a DM claim does. If the DM removes the member while the grant is being written, the grant is given up and the ticket put back, with the same answer, so the device can still join as someone new. It writes a `player` grant.

Players share one password and trust each other, so "continue as" needs no extra proof. Anyone with the password can therefore act as another player and read that player's whispers. The project owner accepted that trust.

**Player link.** The DM hands out one link instead of a room ID and a password: `/r/<roomId>#pw=<password>`, with the password URL-encoded (`shared/playerLink.ts` builds and reads it).

- The password goes in the fragment, not a query parameter, for the same reason as the DM key. A fragment never reaches the server in a request line, a `Referer` or a log, so it never lands in the server's or Cloudflare's logs.
- The client reads `#pw=` and removes it with `history.replaceState` before the app renders, so the password stays neither in the address bar nor in the page's history entry. It runs again on `hashchange` and `popstate`, for a link opened into a page that is already showing. The password is then held in memory only.
- A device that already holds a grant for the room goes straight in and never sends it. Otherwise the join page sends it once, as if the player had typed it and pressed Next, and moves on to step 2. When the room asks for the join challenge (section 10.3), the check appears and the password goes again once it is solved.
- If the password is refused (the DM changed it, or the link was mangled), the page says the link no longer works and to ask the DM for a new one, and the password field is there to type it. A link whose password cannot be decoded is said to be cut off, and nothing is sent.
- Pasting a player link into the Home page's join field works the same way, without the password ever entering the address bar. This is how a player joins inside an iPhone Home Screen app.
- **The DM can show the link at any time** (the project owner's decision, section 17). Beside the argon2id hash that checks joins, `rooms.player_pw_sealed` holds the password under AES-256-GCM with the server key `ROOM_SECRET_KEY` (section 15.5, `server/auth/playerSecret.ts`): a version byte, a random 12-byte IV, the 16-byte tag and the ciphertext. The room ID and its `player_epoch` are bound in as associated data, so a copy moved to another room's row does not open, and a copy left behind by a password change made without one (an older build after a rollback) fails instead of showing a stale link.
  - It is written in the same transaction as the hash: on room creation, on every password change (after the epoch bump), on import, and by the admin commands that create or import a room.
  - Only `GET /api/rooms/:id/player-link`, for a DM grant of that room, ever opens it. It answers `{playerLink}` with `Cache-Control: no-store`. It is never in the room state, so no snapshot, projection, op, log entry or export carries it, and it is never logged: a copy that does not open logs only that fact and the room ID.
  - `playerLink` is null for a room whose password was set before the sealed copy existed, and when the copy does not open because the key changed. The DM panel then says to change the password once, which stores a fresh copy.
  - The DM panel and the Members page have a "Player link" section whose "Show the player link" fetches it and shows copy, share, the QR code and a collapsed "Show password", until the DM hides it again. The room menu's join link copies it for a DM ("Copy player link"), fetched each time the menu's own page shows, because Safari lets a page write the clipboard only straight from the tap. A password change sends every DM socket in the room the `access` signal (section 7.1), so the menu fetches the link again, and a player link shown or still loading on the Members page is fetched again too. A device that reconnects fetches again as well, since the signal may have come while it was offline. Each fetch is numbered and only the newest answer is shown, so an answer from before the change, landing late, never puts the old password on screen. The menu never copies a password that stopped working, whichever DM device changed it. Players, and a DM with no stored copy, copy the plain room link.
  - The cost of keeping the link viewable: anyone who can read both the database and the server's environment can read every player password. A database dump or backup alone cannot. Such a reader could already read every room's state.
- The link is a secret exactly as the password is: anyone who holds it can join. Changing the password stops every old link.

**DM access:**

- `POST /api/rooms/:id/dm` with `{key}` checks the key against `dm_key_hash`.
  - The client reads the key from `location.hash`, posts it, then calls `history.replaceState` to remove the fragment.
  - Fragments never reach the server in a request line, a `Referer` or a log.
  - On success the session token is rotated, and the response sets the new cookie.
  - If the session already has a player grant in the room, its member becomes a DM member: `kind` changes to `'dm'` and this session's grant becomes `dm`. That member's other sessions keep their player grants and stay players.
  - Otherwise the device chooses who it is (owner decision for UD-6: one DM on two devices must stay one member, or whispers and undo split between ghost members). The request carries `memberId`, one of the room's current DM members ("This is me"), or a display `name` for someone new, and a new DM member is created only for a name. With neither, the answer is `409 choose_member` with `{roomId, dmMembers}`, the current DM members' IDs, names and colors in join order, and nothing is written. Picking a member writes a DM grant for that member on this device. The DM key already grants full DM rights. It also opens that member's whispers, which the owner accepted, since whoever holds the key is trusted as the DM. A `memberId` whose member is not a DM, a player or a former DM, gets `403 not_a_dm`, so a player's whispers never come within reach of the DM key. A member no longer in the room gets `409 member_removed`. Both refusals come before anything is spent.
  - After "Replace the DM link", the replacing device keeps its member a DM, so the same person opening the new link on another device picks their own member again.
- **Add a DM device.** Any DM device can mint a one-time device invite: 32 random bytes, stored hashed in `dm_invites` with the room, the minting session, the current `dm_epoch` and an expiry 10 minutes away. It is shown as a QR code and a link (`/r/<roomId>#invite=<token>`).
  - Redemption is one statement: `update dm_invites set used_at = now() where token_hash = $1 and room_id = $2 and used_at is null and expires_at > now() and dm_epoch = (select dm_epoch from rooms where id = $2) returning room_id`. An invite from another room, a used one, an expired one, or one from before a link replacement does nothing.
  - A bare invite token (typed without its link) goes to `POST /api/dm/redeem`, which looks up the invite by `token_hash` (the table's primary key) to find its room, then runs the same statement. It has the same CSRF rule and the same redemption limits as the room route. A DM key always needs the full link, because it is stored per room.
  - Redemption then behaves exactly like a DM claim: it rotates the session, upgrades the session's existing player member in that room, or continues as the chosen DM member, or creates a DM member with a display name, and writes a DM grant, without changing the DM key. The `choose_member` question is answered before the redemption statement runs, so asking never spends the invite.
- **Enter a DM link or invite.** The join page and the Home page have an "Enter a DM link or invite" field that accepts a full link or a bare token. iPhone Home Screen apps keep their own cookie storage and open links in Safari, so this is how a DM signs in inside the installed app. The Home page also suggests joining before adding the app to the Home Screen. A full link opens the claim page as if it were opened. A bare token is first sent without a choice: a device already in the room is signed in, and any other learns the room from the `choose_member` answer and goes on to the claim page with the token as an invite link, to pick who it is there.
- **A DM device that loses DM** (signed out from the devices list, cut off by a replaced link, or DM removed) gets DM wording when its socket closes with `4001` or `4003`: what may have happened, to open the DM link again or an invite from another DM device, the DM link field on the spot, and "Join as a player". The join page of a room this device was a DM in (from the Home page's list, section 14.1) leads with "Were you the DM?" and the DM link field.
- **DM devices.** A DM-only panel lists every session with a DM grant in this room (device label from the user agent, online now or last seen) and can revoke any of them. A session with a socket open in the room counts as online, and closing a socket stamps the session's `last_seen_at`, since a socket makes no HTTP request in its whole life. The list asks again when presence changes and on the `access` signal (section 7.1), which the server sends every DM socket in the room when a DM grant is written (a DM claim or a redeemed invite) or removed. It never polls. Presence alone would miss a device whose member is already online on another one. The response lists this session's own invites of the last hour, so the invite card can say when its invite was used. Revoking deletes only that session's DM grant in this room, closes its sockets at once with `4001`, and deletes the unused invites that session minted. If the member has no DM grant left, its `kind` returns to `'player'`.
- **Remove DM.** A DM can remove another member's DM role. Every DM grant of that member in this room is deleted, its sockets close with `4001`, the unused invites its sessions minted are deleted, and its `kind` returns to `'player'`. The member can rejoin as a player with the password.
- **Replace the DM link** (after a leak) mints a new key, bumps `dm_epoch`, deletes every DM grant except the replacing session's, and sets every other DM member's `kind` back to `'player'`. Their sockets close with `4001`. The epoch bump also voids every outstanding invite.
- **Recovery** when every DM device and the link are lost: on the server, `docker compose -f docker-compose.server.yml exec raccoon node scripts/admin.ts dm-link <roomId>` replaces the link the same way and prints it.
- **Admin commands** in `scripts/admin.ts`, run the same way: `dm-link`, `list-rooms` (last activity and size), `create-room` (bypasses the creation limits, so the server owner is never locked out by a stranger using up the daily count, and prints the player password, the player link and the DM link), `pin` and `unpin` (a pinned room is never unloaded to make space, section 10.5), and `import` (section 16.4). Since v1 wave 5, `hash-password` makes the admin page's password hash (section 15.8), and `import` keeps its temporary files in a folder of its own, so running it inside the live container never deletes an upload in progress. The CLI's pin runs in a process of its own, so it reaches a loaded room only when the room next loads, while the admin page's pin reaches it at once.

**Kicking.** The DM kicks a member, which deletes that member's grants on every session, deletes every unused invite those sessions minted, and closes their sockets with `4001`. A kicked DM member's `kind` returns to `'player'`. Every way of removing a DM grant (kick, device revoke, remove DM, replace link) goes through this same cleanup. The member stays in the roster unless the DM also removes it. Removal kicks first, then deletes the member from the roster, drops it from token and character owners and from handout recipients, closes a handout left with no recipients, and deletes its drawings and areas. Log entries stay, and one more records the removal. A kicked player who knows the password can rejoin, so keeping someone out means changing the password.

**Changing the player password** bumps `player_epoch`, which signs out every player and stops every old player link, and stores the new sealed copy. DM grants are unaffected. The DM is then shown the new player link to send.

**Table displays** (v1 wave 5, docs/design-v1-dm-tools.md 3.1). A TV gets a grant of its own, so it holds exactly what a player who owns nothing holds and never a DM's data. Section 14.14 has the client.

- **Codes.** `POST /api/rooms/:id/display/code` (a DM grant through the room cookie) mints 10 Crockford base32 characters (50 bits), stored as `sha256` in `display_codes` with the room, the minting session, the room's `player_epoch` and an expiry 10 minutes away. At most 3 live codes per room, else `429 too_many_codes`. Revoking the DM device that minted them deletes its unused codes.
- **Redeeming.** `POST /api/display/redeem {code}` takes any caller, with any cookie or none, and reads a code typed in lowercase or with spaces and hyphens. It finds the room by the code's hash, refuses a room with 4 displays with `409 displays_full` before the code is spent, then redeems with one statement, as DM invites do, gated on the room's `player_epoch`. A wrong, used or expired code gets `403 bad_code` and counts in a failure bucket of its own per address, 20 per 10 minutes with the joins' growing delays, so a TV's typos never spend the players' join budget at the same home address. A device that is already that room's display re-pairs without counting against the 4.
- **Display sessions are not room sessions.** A display session is a `sessions` row whose `purpose` is `'display'`, in a cookie of its own, `__Host-rvtt-display` (`rvtt-display` in development), so a DM's laptop can hold both. The server decides by the session's purpose, never by the cookie's name. The display routes (`display/me`, the display image route, Leave and the socket opened with `as=display`) take only a display session from the display cookie. Every other route and socket reads only the room cookie and treats a display session there as no session at all (`401 no_session`), so a display token copied into the room cookie cannot upload, search, join, claim DM, export, tap the X-card or send anything.
- **The grant** is a `grants` row with role `display`, a member id `display:<12 random characters>` that no member can have, and epoch `player_epoch`. Changing the player password signs every display out. The DM devices list shows each as "Table display", and its Sign out deletes the grant. Kicks and member removal never name it, since it is no member.
- **Lifetime.** Leave (`DELETE /api/display/session`) deletes the grant and the session, clears the cookie and closes the display's sockets with `4001`. Otherwise a display session ends 30 days after its last socket closed, not 180 days after use: while a socket is open, the 60 s grant recheck renews its expiry to 30 days ahead whenever it is under 29 days away, and the last socket's close sets it to 30 days from then. Both apply only to a session still live, in the update's own condition, and a close with `4001` renews nothing. The recheck closes a display socket whose session is gone or expired with `4001`.
- **The socket** (`/ws?room=<id>&as=display`) keeps the role `'player'`, with a `display` flag read only where a display differs (section 7.6): its snapshot's `you.role` reads `'display'`, it is never in `online`, and it counts toward the per-room socket cap.

### 10.3 Client IPs and rate limits

- One function, `clientIp(request)`, is the only source of a client address. It returns `CF-Connecting-IP` when `TRUST_CF_CONNECTING_IP=1`, which is safe only while the front end is the only way in and overwrites that header with the real client address, as Cloudflare does (another proxy must be set to do the same). Otherwise set it to 0, and it returns the socket address. The value is truncated to 45 characters, the longest form of an IPv6 address. Every limiter uses it, because behind a proxy or tunnel the raw socket address is always that proxy's container.
- A group that plays in person shares one IP across every device at the table. Everyday limits are therefore keyed on the session when there is one. Because a person who knows a password can mint new sessions, every expensive action also has a per-IP and a per-room limit that new sessions do not reset:
  - **Everything authenticated:** 600 requests per minute per session. Static files are exempt. Image reads have their own limit of 3,000 per minute per session. Requests are classified by the route they reach, never the raw URL, so a percent-encoded path cannot skip a limit.
  - **Unauthenticated:** 120 requests per minute per IP.
  - **Room creation:** section 10.1.
  - **Successful joins, DM claims and invite redemptions:** 30 per hour per IP, and 60 per hour per room.
  - **Join and DM claim failures:** 20 per 10 minutes per IP per room, with an IPv6 address counted by its /64. After that, delays grow exponentially up to a 15-minute lockout for that IP on that room. There is no per-room cap or lockout on failures (the project owner's decision, section 17): a cap shared by every IP would let anyone who knows a room ID lock its new devices and the DM link out for the hour. A device that already holds a grant never needs the password, so a lockout never removes a player who is already in. The cost without the join challenge below, which the project owner accepted: one /64 gets about 60 to 70 guesses an hour on a room, but someone who controls many /64s (one /48 holds 65,536) and knows the room ID is bounded only by the argon2 queue below, about 700,000 guesses an hour at 2 hashes of about 9 ms. That finds a generated three-word passphrase in months (a median near four months at laptop speed, longer on a slower server), and while it runs, joins, room creations and password changes get `429 busy`. A weaker typed password falls sooner.
  - **Join challenge:** when Turnstile is configured, a room that has had 20 or more wrong player passwords in a sliding hour, from all IPs together, asks every password join for a solved Turnstile check (the project owner's follow-up choice, section 17). It is a challenge, never a lockout.
    - The count is kept in `rate_events` under `fail:room:<roomId>`. Only wrong player passwords count. A DM key is 256 random bits, so a wrong one is a stale link, not a guess.
    - The join route checks the count after the per-IP limits and before the argon2 verify, so a challenged guess never takes a place in the hash queue. A missing token answers `403 turnstile_required` and a rejected one `403 turnstile_failed`. Neither counts as a wrong password. If Cloudflare does not answer, the join gets `503 turnstile_unavailable`, failing closed as room creation does. The token is verified with the visitor's own address from `clientIp()`, not its /64.
    - A join with a solved check goes on as usual: the right password joins, and a wrong one is counted, per IP and for the room, like any other. The per-IP backoff above is unchanged. DM links and invites are never challenged, and a device that already holds a grant never sends the password, so nobody is ever locked out.
    - 20 an hour is far above what a table of phones mistyping a three-word passphrase makes. Anyone who knows a room ID can trigger the challenge with 20 wrong guesses, which costs the group only a usually invisible check on new devices. It lifts an hour after the failures stop.
    - Without `TURNSTILE_SECRET`, nothing asks for the check and joins behave exactly as before, with the cost described above.
  - **Searches:** section 11.3.
  - **URL imports and uploads:** 30 per hour per session, 60 per hour per IP, and 120 per hour per room. Scene thumbnails (v1 wave 3, section 14.10) have a budget of their own, 240 per hour per session and per room, and never spend this one: a DM device uploads one by itself each time the DM leaves a scene it changed, and those had used up the DM's map uploads during a long prep session. Only a DM can upload a thumbnail, and each still counts against the room's storage quota. Handouts have one of their own too, 250 per hour per session and per room, since each page of a PDF is one upload (docs/design-v1-pdf-handouts.md 3.7). A URL import spends this budget whatever kind it asks for.
  - **Room exports:** at most 1 in flight per room, 1 per IP and 2 across the server, and 4 per hour per room and per IP. An export is aborted if its reader averages under 64 KiB per second over any 30 s, or if it runs past 30 minutes, so a slow reader cannot hold an export slot.
  - **Outbound fetches** (URL import and its fallback) run at most 4 at a time across the whole server. A request that finds all 4 busy gets `503` with `Retry-After` before anything is downloaded.
  - **Per member, since v1 wave 5** (docs/design-v1-dm-tools.md 2.4). At an in-person table every phone reaches the server from one home address, so the limits wave 5 adds for what people at the table spend count per member and room, and per-address limits stay only as loose caps against a flood, sized for about 8 devices:
    - Session log listings and exports (section 14.16): 10 an hour per member per room, every request counted alike, cached or not, so the remaining count never tells a player that hidden ops moved the room's seq. One export at a time per room (`429 busy`), and 60 an hour per address.
    - Openverse searches: section 11.3.
    - The X-card: in memory only, never in `rate_events`. A tap within 10 s of the card last shown in its room merges into that card, and one session sends at most 6 an hour, then `429`.
    - Wrong table display codes: their own bucket per address (section 10.2).
  - **Rolls and applied changes, since v1 wave 7** (docs/design-v1-dice.md 4.4), in memory: every tagged op spends a member's budget, one token a roll and one for any other, a player's 20 then one every 2 s and a DM's 100 then two a second, refused with `quota`, "You are going too fast. Wait a few seconds." The receipt lookup (section 10.8): 60 an hour per member and room, in `rate_events`.
  - **Admin login** (v1 wave 5, section 15.8), in `rate_events`: 5 failures in 15 minutes, or 10 in an hour, from one address start a 60 s wait, which doubles with each later failure up to an hour. 50 failures in an hour across the site close the login until an hour after the latest one. A stranger can keep the server owner off the page that long, never out of the admin CLI.
- Argon2 verification runs behind a semaphore of 2 concurrent hashes with a queue of at most 8. Beyond the queue the answer is `429`, so password floods cannot starve the event loop or the libuv pool that sharp also uses.
- Slow limit state (rooms per day, lockouts, the join challenge count) is kept in `rate_events`, so a restart does not reset it. Rows older than one day are deleted hourly.

### 10.4 WebSocket security

- Exact `Origin` match against `PUBLIC_ORIGIN` on upgrade, which stops cross-site WebSocket hijacking.
- The session cookie and a valid grant for `?room=` are required before the upgrade completes.
- At most 8 sockets per session, 64 per room, 16 per IP, and 400 on the whole server. A table display's socket (v1 wave 5) counts like any other, and one that sends anything but `hello` or `ping` is closed with `4003` (section 7.6).
- **Snapshot budget.** Every snapshot the server builds (a new socket, an answer to `resync`, a DM's `scene` switch) counts against one budget: 20 snapshots and 60 MiB per minute per session, and 40 snapshots and 120 MiB per minute per IP, ten and twenty of the largest DM snapshot (6 MiB, docs/design-v1-companion-stat-blocks.md 3.4). Over budget, the snapshot is delayed until the budget allows it, and the server sends `wait` so the client's snapshot deadline (section 7.5) does not fire. A new `hello` from the same `(session, clientId)` replaces that client's delayed request instead of adding another, so a reconnect never spends the budget twice. A single delayed request never triggers `4008`, however long it waits. Only a session that keeps making new requests (`scene` messages or new sockets) for 30 s while over budget has those sockets closed with `4008`. For a pinned room, the per-IP part is counted per session, because a whole table playing in person shares one IP. Normal play, including a DM flipping between scenes and a table of phones waking together, stays far below this.
- Ops are limited to 20 per second per socket with a burst of 60, and to 60 per second per room across all sockets. Going over answers `retry` with `busy`, so a busy table never loses an op. A socket that stays over three times its limit for 10 s is closed with `4008`.
- A socket whose `bufferedAmount` stays above 7 MiB for 10 s is closed, so one stalled phone cannot grow server memory. That is 1 MiB over the largest DM snapshot, so one snapshot alone never closes a slow socket.

### 10.5 Room limits

Open room creation means every room must be bounded. The actor checks these before accepting an op and rejects with `quota` beyond them. A room whose stored size reaches its quota becomes read-only except for deletions.

| Item | Limit |
| --- | --- |
| Scenes | 50 per room |
| Scene bounds | 200 x 200 cells |
| Tokens | 500 per scene |
| Characters | 50 per room |
| Members | 50 per room |
| Templates | 200 per scene |
| Drawings | 2,000 per scene, 2,000 point values each, 100,000 point values per scene in total |
| Labels | 500 per scene |
| Build | the scene bounds; `build.paint`, `build.rect` and `build.poly` change at most 2,000 cells per op |
| Fog draft | 500 ops per scene |
| Handouts | 200 per room |
| Handout pages (PDF handouts) | 100 per handout and per document. 2,000 per room, counting every page of every document and of every handout showing, one image as one page, so a document shown counts its pages again while it shows. That keeps the documents in a DM snapshot to about 200 KB (docs/design-v1-pdf-handouts.md 3.7). |
| Custom monsters | 200 per room, 16 KiB each serialized, 1 MiB in all. A create op is bounded by the protocol, and `checkLimits` measures what is stored, because edits can grow a monster and undo can bring one back. Each monster's size is measured once per version (entities are replaced, never changed), so the check costs nothing on ops that do not touch one. They ride in every DM snapshot, so they count toward the 3 MiB a DM's projected scene may hold. |
| Saved encounters | 100 per room. One holds at most 20 kinds of monster and 50 creatures, so placing it (one create op per creature) stays inside a socket's op burst of 60 (section 10.4), and a party of at most 10 levels. They ride in every DM snapshot: at most about 2.5 KB each, under 256 KB in all. |
| SRD monster images | 400 per room, more than the SRD's 330 monsters. The reducer checks only the shape of an SRD ID (a lowercase slug, at most 80 characters), because the SRD list lives in a client chunk of its own, so the count is what bounds them. |
| Log | 200 in memory, 10,000 rows stored, oldest trimmed |
| Serialized room state | 8 MiB |
| Serialized projected scene | 3 MiB |
| Areas | size, width and height 5 to 1,000 ft |
| Custom token size | 0.5 to 20 cells |
| Free walls (v1 wave 3) | 4,000 per scene, each at most 8 cells long, 500 and 48 KiB serialized per op. 500 doors serialize to about 73 KB, past the 64 KiB frame, so the byte bound is what binds. |
| The split's candidate pairs (v1 wave 3) | 100,000 per scene, every scene (section 9.7) |
| Pieces the split adds (v1 wave 3) | 5,000 per scene, every scene (section 9.7) |
| Pillars (v1 wave 3) | 1,000 pillar cells per scene. Each is four stored wall segments, so a pillar in every cell would be 160,000, past the geometry budgets. A room already over the cap keeps its pillars: the limits step refuses only an op, undo included, that raises the scene's count past it. A room import refuses a scene over it (section 9.7). |
| Doors and windows (v1 wave 3) | 1,000 per scene, on grid edges and free walls together, secret doors included. Each stays one stored segment, never merged into a run, so before the cap only the 3 MiB scene bounded them. The same rule as pillars: the limits step refuses only an op, undo included, that raises the count past it, and a room import refuses a scene over it (section 9.7). |
| Drawn edges (v1 wave 3) | 20,000 per scene: every edge entry the scene's chunks store, walls, doors, windows and removed automatic walls alike. Each is an entry the derive merges on every derive, so before the cap only the 3 MiB scene bounded them. The same rule as pillars (section 9.7). |
| Stamps (v1 wave 3) | 500 per scene, 100 per op, each 0.25 to 20 cells a side |
| Lights (v1 wave 3) | 100 per scene, 100 per op. Bright at most 120 ft, and dim at most 120 ft beyond it: the SRD's largest light, Daylight, is 60 ft bright and 60 ft dim beyond. Sight (v1 wave 4) also stops each radius at 24 cells, 120 ft at 5 ft per cell, so a finer grid never makes a light sweep more cells and walls than the server-box gate measured (addendum 2.4). |
| Map phases (v1 wave 3) | 8 per scene, the active one included |
| Folder name (v1 wave 3) | 40 characters, under the name rules (section 6) |
| Scene thumbnail upload (v1 wave 3) | 512 KiB, stored at most 320 px on its long side |
| Heights and stairs (v1 wave 4) | the scene bounds, 2,000 cells per `build.height` or `build.stairs`, ground from -100 to 200 ft in whole feet |
| Party sight sources (v1 wave 4) | 12 per scene lend sight. Nothing is refused past it: the rest stay shown and lend none (section 9.10). |
| Lit token lights (v1 wave 4) | 20 per scene, counting every token whose light gives any, except tokens the DM hid, whose light is dark for players: "This scene already has 20 lit token lights." A token's light radii are each at most 120 ft, as a free light's. The cap refuses only the DM's op that raises the count past it, the DM showing a lit token included. A player's op, a hidden combatant revealed on its turn and a room import are never refused by it. Past the cap sight lights the first 20 in a fixed order, the party's own first in the source cap's order, then the rest, each by token ID, and the DM's Vision settings say how many light nothing. |
| Senses (v1 wave 4) | darkvision, blindsight and truesight read up to 150 ft, the SRD's longest darkvision, and reach at most 30 cells, which binds only on a grid finer than 5 ft per cell: at 1 ft per cell, capped senses and lights made a room's first snapshot of the densest dark scene take about 368 ms on a development laptop, past the 250 ms budget for the server, and with the cap in cells it costs what it does at 5 ft (addendum 2.4 and 4.3.6). |
| Potential sight pieces (v1 wave 4) | 6,000 per scene with vision on: every piece of the split wall set that would block sight with every door closed and every secret door hidden, and every raised edge whatever its height. Counted whatever the doors' states, so only a build op or turning vision on can reach it, and a player closing a door never gets `quota`. Over it: "This scene has 9,200 wall pieces, more than vision can handle (6,000)." The limits step refuses only an op that raises the count past it, and a room import refuses a scene over it (`shared/visionLimits.ts`). The addendum set 8,000. The server-box gate lowered it, since a room's first snapshot of the densest dark scene took 267 ms on a modest 4-core server against its 250 ms budget (addendum 4.3.6). |
| Journal pages (v1 wave 5) | 200 per room. Title 80 characters, folder 40. At most 100 blocks a page, 4,000 characters a block, 32 KiB serialized a page (an op that carries a page is bounded by the same number), and 1 MiB for every page together. Pages are room level, so they ride in every DM snapshot and count toward a DM's projected scene, as custom monsters do. Each page is measured once per version. |
| Map pins (v1 wave 5) | 200 per scene, label 80 characters |
| Roll requests (v1 wave 5) | 20 open in a room at once, 1 to 50 creatures each, 1 or 2 choices each, note 80 characters, DC 1 to 50, a total -50 to 100 |
| Group ops (v1 wave 5) | 100 tokens per `token.updateMany` or `token.deleteMany`, 100 adds and 100 removals per `actor.effects`. A larger selection goes out in batches under one undo group. |
| Table displays (v1 wave 5) | 4 per room, 3 live codes per room, each valid 10 minutes and once (section 10.2) |
| Dice (v1 wave 7) | 20 rolls, 100 dice after each mode and 3 KiB per `dice.roll` (a larger group goes in batches under one tag), 20 parts, sides 2 to 1,000, numbers to 1,000, a formula's canonical text 100 characters, a label 60, an extra 40. A request's rolled answer must range within -50 to 100. Receipts: 10,000 a room younger than 24 hours, 2,000 a player, the players together 8,000 (docs/design-v1-dice.md 1.2 and 4.4) |
| Loaded rooms | 50 in memory, and at most 10 from rooms created from one IP. When another room must load, the server unloads an unpinned room that has been loaded for at least 10 minutes and has had no op for at least 10 minutes, oldest first, even if it has sockets: they close with `4010` and wait 30 s before reconnecting. If no room qualifies, the load is refused and its sockets close with `4011` to retry with backoff. Because a newly loaded room cannot be evicted for 10 minutes, two idle rooms cannot take turns evicting each other. A pinned room (admin `pin`) is never unloaded, and when it must load it may unload the oldest unpinned room regardless of idle time. |
